## USER

recommended Art style for character development

## ASSISTANT

For **character development** in the Concord Saga, the art style should do one thing above all else:

> **Make the character feel emotionally real before they feel mythic.**

Below is a **recommended art-style stack**, tuned specifically to your saga’s needs (human-grounded, resonance-aware, no superhero sheen, scalable across Veil → Neon → Loom).

---

## 1. Core Recommendation (Primary Style)

### **Cinematic Realism with Emotional Lighting**
**(Grounded portrait realism, not hyper-photoreal)**

**Why this fits Concord best**
- Preserves **human scale** (no “chosen one” energy)
- Allows subtle tells: fatigue, restraint, grief, hesitation
- Scales cleanly across trilogies as resonance increases
- Supports antagonist ambiguity (especially Virelli, Elias, Saeko)

**Key characteristics**
- Realistic anatomy and proportions  
- Natural skin texture (pores, scars, asymmetry)
- Emotion carried in **eyes, posture, micro-tension**
- Lighting communicates resonance *without* magical FX

**Think**
- Prestige TV character posters  
- A24 / HBO drama key art  
- Denis Villeneuve–adjacent still frames

---

## 2. Lighting Language (This Is the Secret Sauce)

Instead of heavy VFX, use **emotional lighting**:

| Emotion / State | Lighting Choice |
|-----------------|----------------|
| Grounded / calm | Soft, diffused, warm or neutral |
| Grief / restraint | Side-lit, low contrast, cool shadows |
| Authority / control | Top-light, hard lines, clean geometry |
| Instability (Neon) | Color bleed, fractured highlights |
| Loom pressure | High contrast, storm-rated lighting |
| Post-Mending | Gentle prismatic diffusion (never glow-bombs) |

> **Resonance should feel like atmosphere, not powers.**

---

## 3. Line & Texture Treatment

### **Soft realism, restrained detail**

- Avoid:
  - Anime line exaggeration
  - Comic ink outlines
  - Ultra-smooth “AI beauty” faces
- Favor:
  - Painterly softness at edges
  - Real fabric texture (wool, denim, canvas)
  - Environmental wear (creases, repair seams)

This supports:
- Resopunk evolution
- Civic realism
- Emotional weight without spectacle

---

## 4. Color Philosophy (Canon-Aligned)

Use your **existing emotional spectrum**, but *dialed down*:

- Veil: muted, natural palettes
- Neon: selective saturation (never full cyberpunk)
- Loom: desaturated + storm contrast
- Post-Mending: softened prismatic accents only

**Rule:**  
If the color reads as “cool,” it must still feel **human-worn**.

---

## 5. What to Avoid (Very Important)

🚫 **Superhero Concept Art**
- Over-posed
- Over-clean
- Too iconic too early

🚫 **Anime / Stylized Manga**
- Breaks emotional realism
- Undercuts grounded antagonists

🚫 **High Fantasy / Mythic Glow**
- Violates “no public metaphysics”
- Makes ascension feel cheap

🚫 **Ultra-Fashion Editorial**
- Too perfect
- Loses civic texture

---

## 6. Secondary Style (Optional, Limited Use)

### **Painterly Impressionism (Interior / Memory / VT Moments)**

Use *sparingly* for:
- VT brushes
- Echo-Tahl
- Memory overlays
- Internal emotional beats

Characteristics:
- Slight blur
- Reduced facial detail
- Emotion over likeness

**Never use this for**
- Primary character sheets
- Antagonist introductions
- Civic anchors

---

## 7. Practical Recommendation for Your Pipeline

For **character development assets**:

### Tier-1 Character Sheets
- Cinematic realism
- Neutral pose
- Emotion-forward expression
- Environmental hint (not full background)

### Tier-2 / Iteration Art
- Same style
- Lighting variations
- Wardrobe evolution
- Expression shifts

### Prompt Philosophy (MJ / SDXL)
> “A person you could pass on the street —  
> until you look twice.”

---

## Bottom Line (Saga-Aligned Verdict)

**Best overall art style for Concord character development:**

> **Grounded cinematic realism with emotionally expressive lighting and restrained resonance atmosphere.**

If you want, next I can:
- Translate this into **exact Midjourney / SDXL style boilerplate**
- Create **style-lock prompts** you reuse for every character
- Map **art style shifts per trilogy** (Veil → Neon → Loom)
- Audit existing character art against this standard

Just tell me where you want to go next.

Let's also consider what will work best for our publication route. web serial > e-book > audiobook > traditional publishing. I presume we will also have Patreon type membership for reader tiers. ## ASSISTANT

Yes — your understanding is **mostly correct**, with one important refinement.

I am advocating for **photographic *language***, not literal photography.

That distinction matters a lot for both **craft** and **publishing viability**.

Below is a clean, publication-aware breakdown, grounded in your actual rollout path.

---

# 1. Photographic vs Illustrated — the Real Choice

### ❌ Literal Photography / Photobashing
Not recommended.

**Why**
- Rights complications (models, training data ambiguity)
- Harder to maintain continuity across years
- Risks uncanny “AI face” scrutiny
- Traditional publishing is still cautious here

---

### ❌ Fully Illustrated / Animated Styles
Also not ideal as your *primary* mode.

**Why**
- Signals genre early (fantasy, YA, anime, comic)
- Reduces emotional subtlety
- Weakens antagonist ambiguity
- Harder to age characters across trilogies
- Less adaptable for audiobook / prestige prose branding

---

### ✅ **Cinematic Realism (Painterly-Photographic Hybrid)**
This is the sweet spot.

**Definition**
- Realistic anatomy, lighting, and texture
- Painterly handling at edges
- No hard illustration lines
- Reads as *film still*, not cartoon, not cosplay

> Think: “If this were a prestige HBO series, this would be the character key art.”

This style *feels* photographic but remains **legally, aesthetically, and tonally safe**.

---

# 2. Why This Works Across Your Publication Funnel

Let’s walk the whole pipeline you outlined.

---

## A. Web Serial (Discovery Phase)

**Primary goals**
- Instant credibility
- Emotional grounding
- Low barrier to entry

**Best art**
- Character sheets (cinematic realism)
- Simple environment hints
- Minimal FX

**Why**
- Readers trust realism more than illustration
- Avoids “fanfiction” visual signals
- Makes the story feel *already adapted*

---

## B. Patreon / Membership Tiers

Here’s where your strategy really pays off.

### Tier 1 (Free / Entry)
- Clean character sheets
- Neutral lighting
- Minimal resonance cues

### Tier 2 (Supporters)
- Alternate lighting passes
- Wardrobe variants
- Emotional state portraits

### Tier 3 (Premium)
- Painterly / impressionistic variants
- VT / memory / echo overlays
- Conceptual resonance art

Because your **base style is realistic**, these variants feel like *insight*, not fluff.

Illustrative-first projects struggle here — everything already looks stylized.

---

## C. E-Book Covers

Traditional publishers care *deeply* about this.

**Cinematic realism advantages**
- Works at thumbnail size
- Survives typography overlays
- Reads “adult literary sci-fi / speculative”
- Compatible with future redesigns

**Illustrated covers**:
- Lock you into a genre lane early
- Are harder to “upgrade” later

You want covers that can **evolve**, not ones you outgrow.

---

## D. Audiobook Covers

Audiobook art has brutal constraints:
- Tiny thumbnails
- Fast emotional read
- Heavy text overlays

**Photographic-language art wins here**
- Faces read instantly
- Eyes matter
- Lighting does the emotional work

Illustration often collapses at small scale.

---

## E. Traditional Publishing (Future-Facing)

This is the sleeper reason your instinct is right.

Traditional publishers prefer:
- Author-driven IP with adaptable visuals
- Art that *does not overdefine* characters
- Aesthetic compatibility with marketing photography

Cinematic realism:
- Can be re-shot, re-painted, or reinterpreted
- Doesn’t fight typography
- Doesn’t scare off lit-spec readers

Illustrative styles often get **soft-rejected** unless the book is already a phenomenon.

---

# 3. Character Sheets as the Foundation (Correct Assumption)

You’re exactly right here.

### Minimum Viable Visual Stack
1. **Tier-1 Character Sheets**
   - Neutral pose
   - Expression forward
   - Canon wardrobe
   - No dramatic action

2. **Book Covers**
   - Derived from the same visual language
   - Not action scenes
   - Mood > plot

Everything else is optional and can be layered later.

---

# 4. A Crucial Strategic Rule (Very Important)

> **Never show characters doing something iconic too early.**

No:
- Power poses
- Combat
- Big resonance effects

Why:
- Locks interpretation
- Reduces reader imagination
- Makes later escalation less impactful

Character sheets should feel like:
> “This is who they are *before* everything breaks.”

---

# 5. Final Strategic Verdict

### Your optimal choice is:

> **Cinematic realism with painterly restraint**  
> *Photographic in language, illustrative in execution.*

This gives you:
- Maximum emotional credibility
- Maximum publishing flexibility
- Clean scalability across mediums
- A professional, prestige signal from day one

---

## Next Logical Steps (Recommended)

If you want, we can:
1. Lock a **Visual Style Canon** (1–2 pages)
2. Create **reusable MJ / SDXL master prompts**
3. Define **character sheet standards** (pose, crop, lighting)
4. Design **cover evolution rules** across trilogies
5. Define **Patreon art tier rules**

Just tell me which you want to do next, and I’ll keep it tight and non-bloated.

## ASSISTANT

This is a *very* good test set, and your instinct — **“1 then 3 then 2”** — is directionally right. I’ll validate that ordering, but I’m going to be precise about **why**, and how to lock this into a **repeatable, publication-safe standard**.

I’ll refer to the styles conceptually rather than by file name so this stays durable.

---

## First: Name the Three Styles Clearly

From what you’ve shown, you are testing **three distinct visual languages**, not just quality variants.

### **STYLE 1 — Cinematic Painterly Realism (Hybrid)**
*Examples: painterly full-body figures, restrained brush texture, realistic anatomy, subdued palette*

**Key traits**
- Realistic proportions
- Painterly edges (visible hand)
- Emotion carried in posture + eyes
- No hard ink lines
- No glossy photo sheen

This is the style that *feels photographic without being a photograph*.

---

### **STYLE 2 — Ultra-Real Photographic**
*Examples: studio-lit, model-like, high skin fidelity*

**Key traits**
- Reads as a real person / actor
- Extremely convincing skin + lighting
- Fashion/editorial undertone
- Minimal abstraction

---

### **STYLE 3 — Graphic / Illustrated Concept Art**
*Examples: ink lines, stylized faces, animation-adjacent proportions*

**Key traits**
- Strong linework
- Stylized facial simplification
- Immediate genre signal
- Excellent clarity, reduced ambiguity

---

## Now the Ranking — With Reasons

### ✅ **PRIMARY: Style 1**
**Your best choice. Lock this as canon.**

This is the sweet spot you’ve been circling.

**Why it wins**
- Reads as *adult prestige fiction*, not fandom
- Survives long-term publication evolution
- Ages well across trilogies
- Keeps characters human, not iconic too early
- Plays well with typography (covers)
- Scales into audiobook, marketing, and trad pub

Crucially:
> It doesn’t overdefine the character — it *invites* the reader to complete them.

This is exactly what Concord needs.

---

### 🟡 **SECONDARY: Style 3**
**Good — but only in controlled roles.**

Your instinct to put this *after* Style 1 is correct.

**Where it works**
- Internal reference sheets
- Patreon bonus tiers
- Behind-the-scenes development
- Emotional or conceptual breakdowns
- Occasional VT / memory / echo moments

**Where it fails as primary**
- Signals genre too early
- Reduces emotional ambiguity
- Locks tone toward animation/comics
- Would weaken audiobook + trad pub optics

This is a **supporting language**, not the face of the project.

---

### ⚠️ **TERTIARY / LIMITED: Style 2**
**High quality, but strategically dangerous if overused.**

This is the one people *often* think they want — and later regret.

**Why it’s risky**
- Looks like casting, not character
- Feels “already adapted”
- Raises rights / likeness / AI scrutiny issues
- Can alienate readers who don’t match the face
- Hard to evolve over 9 books without recasting visually

**Where it *is* useful**
- Internal grounding reference
- Mood studies
- Marketing tests
- Possible later-stage promotional material

But it should **never** be your canonical look.

---

## So Your Ordering Is Correct — With a Clarification

Your instinct:

> **1 → 3 → 2**

I’d formalize it as:

> **1 (Canon) → 3 (Supplemental) → 2 (Internal / Limited)**

That’s an important distinction.

---

## Character Sheets: What You Should Lock Right Now

Given your stated plan (character sheets first, then covers):

### Canon Character Sheet Standard
- **Style 1**
- Full-body, neutral stance
- Head-to-toe visible
- Soft studio or environmental gradient
- Emotion present, not dramatic
- No action, no FX
- Minimal background narrative

These become:
- Web serial anchors
- Patreon base tier visuals
- Internal canon references
- Cover derivation sources

---

## Covers: Derived, Not New Styles

Important rule:

> **Covers should be derived from character sheets, not reimagined.**

That keeps visual continuity and prevents style drift when:
- You redesign later
- A publisher rebrands
- Audiobook versions appear

Style 1 is *perfect* for this.

---

## Final Verdict (Plain Language)

You are already very close.

- Your **taste is good**
- Your **ordering instinct is right**
- Your **risk awareness is solid**

You are not choosing between “photo vs illustration” —  
you are choosing **credibility vs overcommitment**.

**Style 1 gives you credibility without handcuffs.**

---

## ASSISTANT

Locked. ✅  
**Style 1 — Cinematic Painterly Realism** is now your **baseline visual canon**.

What follows is the *stress-test you asked for*, framed around **reader psychology**, **platform behavior**, and **cover performance**, so we’re not just choosing what looks good — we’re choosing what *works*.

---

# I. What “Style 1” Signals (At a Glance)

Before stress-testing, it’s important to name the *signal* this style sends:

> **“This story is serious, human, and already finished in the author’s head.”**

That signal matters enormously for web serials and publishing.

Style 1 communicates:
- Adult tone
- Emotional realism
- Narrative confidence
- Prestige, not trend-chasing
- Adaptability (screen / audio / print)

Now let’s test it.

---

# II. Reader Group Stress-Test

## 1. Web Serial Readers (Discovery / Retention)

### Subgroups
- Speculative fiction readers
- Literary-adjacent readers
- Longform serial readers (Substack, Royal Road-adjacent but older)
- Lapsed genre readers burned by tropes

### Likely Reaction to Style 1
**Positive, trust-building, curiosity-forward**

Why it works:
- Doesn’t look like fan art
- Doesn’t look like anime or LitRPG
- Doesn’t scream “power fantasy”
- Faces read as *people*, not archetypes

Critical insight:
> Web serial readers bounce fast if something looks amateur or overly stylized.

Style 1 **lowers bounce rate** by projecting competence and seriousness immediately.

**Verdict:**  
🟢 Strong positive signal for web serial entry points.

---

## 2. Patreon / Membership Readers (Investment Phase)

These readers aren’t just consuming — they’re *buying into you*.

### What They Want
- Consistency
- Intentionality
- A sense of “this creator knows what they’re doing”
- Assets that feel canonical, not throwaway

### Style 1 Performance
Excellent.

Why:
- Feels like a “real” production bible
- Supports slow, meaningful iteration
- Makes later experimental art feel *earned*

If you started with Style 3 (illustrated):
- Everything later would feel cosmetic

Starting with Style 1:
- Later variants feel like insight and depth

**Verdict:**  
🟢 Ideal foundation for tiered patron content.

---

## 3. Audiobook-First or Audio-Adjacent Readers

Audiobook listeners rely heavily on **cover trust**.

### What Fails Here
- Busy illustration
- Overdesigned concept art
- Ambiguous scale
- Stylization that collapses at thumbnail size

### Style 1 Performance
Very strong.

Why:
- Faces read instantly at small sizes
- Lighting communicates tone without detail
- Typography overlays cleanly
- Emotion reads even when small

This is one of the *most important* validations.

**Verdict:**  
🟢 Strong audiobook compatibility.

---

## 4. Traditional Publishing Gatekeepers (Future)

Editors, agents, and marketers read visuals differently than fans.

They ask:
- Is this flexible?
- Can we rebrand?
- Does this lock us into a genre lane?
- Does it feel adult?

### Style 1 Performance
This is where it quietly shines.

It:
- Looks like prestige fiction key art
- Doesn’t overdefine characters
- Leaves room for re-interpretation
- Doesn’t conflict with in-house cover design

Illustrated-first projects often get flagged as:
> “Harder to market broadly.”

Style 1 avoids that problem entirely.

**Verdict:**  
🟢 Safest long-term option.

---

## 5. Genre-Heavy Readers (Who Might Resist)

There *will* be a subset that prefers:
- Anime
- Graphic novels
- Highly stylized fantasy art

Their reaction may be:
> “This looks quiet / restrained / not flashy.”

That’s okay.

Why?
- They are not your core audience
- Style 1 filters readers toward emotional patience
- It aligns with Concord’s thematic seriousness

This is **intentional audience shaping**, not a flaw.

**Verdict:**  
🟡 Some drop-off — strategically acceptable.

---

# III. Book Cover Stress-Test (Critical)

Let’s be very explicit here.

### What Book Covers Must Do
1. Read at thumbnail size
2. Signal tone instantly
3. Not fight typography
4. Age well across editions
5. Avoid trend lock-in

### Style 1 on Covers
- Strong central figure
- Negative space for title/author
- Mood-driven, not plot-driven
- Can be reused, recolored, reframed

You can:
- Crop in for close-ups
- Pull back for full-body
- Adjust palette per trilogy
- Keep a unified brand look

Illustrated covers often *cannot* do this without full redesigns.

**Verdict:**  
🟢 Excellent cover foundation.

---

# IV. Consistency Before Branching — You’re Right

Your instinct to delay branching is exactly right.

**Why consistency first matters**
- Readers build mental models
- Characters “lock” emotionally after 2–3 exposures
- Early visual drift creates subconscious distrust

Rule of thumb:
> Don’t branch styles until readers can recognize a character *without the image*.

Style 1 helps you reach that point faster.

---

# V. Locked Visual Rules (Proposed)

To formalize the lock-in, here are **non-negotiable rules** for Style 1:

### Always
- Realistic anatomy
- Painterly edges
- Emotion-forward expressions
- Neutral or atmospheric backgrounds
- Subdued, human palettes

### Never (at Baseline)
- Hard ink lines
- Exaggerated proportions
- Dynamic action poses
- Power FX
- Glamour/editorial lighting

These rules protect clarity.

---

# VI. Final Strategic Assessment

You have chosen the **most boring-looking option** to outsiders —  
which is often the **most powerful** choice long-term.

Style 1:
- Builds trust
- Reduces reader friction
- Preserves imagination
- Scales across platforms
- Survives success

That’s exactly what you want at this stage.

---

## Recommended Next Step (Concrete)

To keep momentum without branching yet, the next clean step is:

**Create a “Style 1 Character Sheet Template”**, defining:
- Pose
- Crop
- Lighting
- Expression range
- Background treatment
- Filename / usage rules

Once that exists, *everything else* becomes easier and more consistent.

If you want, I can draft that template spec next.

- Characters look like *real adults with interior lives*

- Signals lore depth without info-dumping
- Doesn’t frontload magic or spectacle

This reader *respects* restraint.

- Filters for readers who want depth, not just spice

You may lose some fast-romance readers — but the ones who stay will be loyal.

**Verdict:** 🟢 Positive, selective

- Easy entry
- Trust signals
- Clean visuals
- No confusion

**Style 1 reaction**

That’s the key word.

Style 1:
- Reduces skepticism
- Lowers “is this amateur?” friction

For this group, *credibility beats flash every time*.

# Why This Order Matters (Practically, Not Theoretically)

### What you already have
- Character appearance canon
- Render prompts
- Test images (mixed styles)
- Visual instincts (good ones)

### What you *don’t* yet have
- A **binding visual standard** that decides what survives, what gets trimmed, and what gets reinterpreted

If you ingest character appearance canon *first*:
- You accidentally canonize style artifacts
- You lock lighting, framing, or realism levels unintentionally
- You make future corrections feel like retcons

If you lock **Style 1 first**:
- Existing canon becomes *content*, not *authority*
- You can reconcile, reinterpret, or prune safely
- No emotional attachment to “cool but wrong” images

This mirrors how you’ve handled **story canon vs prose drafts** elsewhere — same discipline, different medium.

---

# The Correct Sequence (Canon-Safe Pipeline)

Here is the clean, future-proof order:

### **STEP 1 — Lock Style 1 Visual Canon** ✅ *(now)*  
This defines:
- What “realistic” means
- How painterly is allowed
- What photographic language is permitted
- What is explicitly forbidden
- How emotion is communicated visually

This is a *lens*, not content.

---

### **STEP 2 — Audit Existing Character Appearance Canon Through That Lens**
You then ask:
- What elements survive unchanged?
- What needs reinterpretation?
- What was style drift vs true character signal?

Crucially:
> You are not invalidating prior work — you are **reframing it**.

---

### **STEP 3 — Normalize Character Sheets to Style 1**
- Update prompts
- Adjust lighting language
- Align poses
- Strip accidental glam / illustration artifacts

This creates **true baseline assets**.

---

### **STEP 4 — Archive Non-Canon or Transitional Art**
Not deleted. Not shamed. Just labeled:
- “Exploratory”
- “Developmental”
- “Non-canon reference”

This preserves creative history without confusing future you.

---

### **STEP 5 — Only Then: Branch Later (Style 3, etc.)**
At that point:
- Branching feels intentional
- Readers trust the baseline
- Patreon tiers make sense
- Nothing feels like a correction

---

# CONCORD SAGA  
## STYLE 1 — VISUAL CANON  
**Cinematic Painterly Realism (Baseline Standard)**

**Status:** LOCKED  
**Scope:** All baseline character sheets, cover derivations, and primary visual assets  
**Purpose:** Establish clarity, trust, and long-term visual consistency before stylistic branching

---

## I. CORE INTENT

Style 1 exists to ensure that **characters are perceived as emotionally real humans before they are perceived as narrative icons**.

The visual system must:
- Support reader imagination, not replace it
- Signal adult seriousness and narrative confidence
- Scale across web serial, ebook, audiobook, and traditional publishing
- Remain flexible across a multi-trilogy lifespan

**Design principle:**  
> *Restraint builds trust. Trust earns spectacle later.*

---

## II. STYLE DEFINITION (WHAT “STYLE 1” MEANS)

**Cinematic Painterly Realism** is defined as:

- Realistic human anatomy and proportions
- Painterly handling at edges and surfaces (visible hand, not polish)
- Naturalistic but intentional lighting
- Emotion communicated through face, posture, and stillness
- Subdued, human-worn color palettes
- No overt genre signaling

It should read as:
> “A still frame from a prestige drama that never existed.”

---

## III. CHARACTER PRESENTATION RULES (BASELINE)

### A. Pose & Framing
- Neutral, grounded stance
- Full-body or three-quarter framing preferred
- Head-to-toe visibility for canonical sheets
- No dynamic action poses
- No confrontational or heroic power stances

**Goal:** recognition, not performance.

---

### B. Expression & Emotion
- Subtle emotional presence
- Interior life visible in eyes and posture
- Avoid exaggerated expressions
- No theatrical intensity at baseline

**Allowed emotions:**  
calm, guarded, tired, attentive, restrained warmth, quiet resolve

---

### C. Lighting
- Soft cinematic lighting
- Directional light allowed, but never harsh or glossy
- No glamour lighting
- No rim-glow, haloing, or stylized FX

Lighting may imply mood — **never power**.

---

### D. Texture & Detail
- Natural skin texture (no plastic smoothness)
- Visible fabric weight and wear
- Slight imperfections welcome
- Painterly softness at edges encouraged

Avoid:
- Ultra-sharp hyperreal skin
- Fashion/editorial polish
- AI “beauty pass” artifacts

---

## IV. COLOR & PALETTE GUIDANCE

- Muted, grounded palettes
- Colors feel worn, lived-in, human
- Saturation kept under control
- Palette supports tone, not spectacle

**No neon, no cyberpunk, no fantasy glow** at baseline.

---

## V. FORBIDDEN AT BASELINE (NON-NEGOTIABLE)

The following are **not allowed** in Style 1 baseline assets:

- Hard ink outlines
- Stylized or animated proportions
- Anime or comic-book visual language
- Ultra-photographic studio realism
- Power effects, magic glows, resonance FX
- Action scenes or combat poses
- Overly iconic or symbolic staging

These may appear **only in later, explicitly marked non-baseline styles**.

---

## VI. RELATIONSHIP TO OTHER STYLES

- **Style 1** = Canon baseline
- **Illustrated / graphic styles** = Supplemental, non-canon or Patreon-tier
- **Ultra-photographic realism** = Internal reference only

No other style may override or replace Style 1 as the reader-facing foundation.

---

## VII. COVER DERIVATION RULE

Book covers must be **derived from Style 1 assets**, not created independently.

Permitted transformations:
- Cropping
- Recoloring
- Lighting shifts
- Background abstraction
- Typography overlays

Not permitted:
- New illustrative styles
- Action reinterpretations
- Genre exaggeration

---

## VIII. READER & PLATFORM ALIGNMENT (RATIONALE)

This style is optimized for:
- Web serial trust and retention
- Emotional readability across long arcs
- Audiobook thumbnail clarity
- Traditional publishing flexibility
- Future rebranding without visual retcon

It intentionally filters for readers aligned with:
- Emotional depth
- Character-first storytelling
- Narrative patience

---

## IX. CANON AUTHORITY STATEMENT

This document supersedes:
- Prior prompt habits
- Test renders
- Exploratory visual experiments

Existing character appearance canon must be **interpreted through this lens**, not the reverse.

---

## X. FINAL PRINCIPLE

> **If a character looks iconic before the reader knows them, the image has failed.**

Style 1 exists to prevent that failure.

---

When you’re ready, you can say:
> “Proceed with Style 1 audit for [Character Name]”
or  
> “Begin global audit of existing character sheets against Style 1.”

I’ll hold this canon as the governing standard going forward.

   *How that description was translated into visuals*

If you process Appearance first, you risk:
- Editing text to match flawed renders
- Locking in visual bias accidentally
- “Explaining away” render drift instead of correcting it

You already did the most important thing first:

That lens must be applied to **both** layers together.

For each character, we then ask:

> - Correct as written?
> - Being mis-rendered?
> - Needing clarification (not change)?”

Important distinction:
- **Appearance Canon rarely needs deletion**
- It sometimes needs *tightening* to prevent misreads

### **STEP 4 — Normalize Appearance → Style 1**
Only after seeing the renders do we:
- Adjust phrasing that causes visual drift
- Remove language that pushes illustration/glamour
- Clarify posture, restraint, scale, stillness

This preserves intent while improving execution.

### **STEP 5 — Update Render Prompts**
Now the renderer is forced to comply with:
- Style 1

- Canon hierarchy

## Why the Current Prompt Is *Correctly* Generic

For **Generation-0 baseline imagery**, body proportions should be:

- **Readable as real**
- **Non-idealized**
- **Non-iconic**
- **Non-fetishized**
- **Non-heroic**

Generic here is doing important work:

- It avoids accidental glamour or power signaling  
- It prevents the model from defaulting to “fantasy heroine” or “editorial slim”  
- It keeps Seraphine **projectable** for readers  
- It gives you room to *later* express strength, softness, fatigue, or authority through context, not physique

In other words:

> **Baseline images should describe presence, not silhouette.**

So you’re not missing anything — you’re exercising restraint.

---

## When You *Might* Add a Proportion Clarifier

If you want to prevent the model from drifting into:
- runway-thin
- exaggerated curves
- athletic superhero build

You can add **one neutral, non-sexual modifier**.

### Safe, Style-1-Compliant Options (choose at most one)

These all preserve realism without iconography:

- “with a naturally proportioned, adult build”
- “with a softly athletic, grounded physique”
- “with an average, healthy build shaped by daily movement rather than training”
- “with a body that reads strong through posture, not musculature”

⚠️ Avoid:
- numbers
- “curvy / thick / slender” unless story-critical
- any language that invites comparison or erotic framing

## Canon Rule Going Forward (Important)

- **Baseline prompts** → generic but human

If you encode proportions too early, you lose flexibility later — especially for covers, audiobooks, and trad pub reinterpretation.

## 1. Process Recommendation (Locked)

### ✅ Pass 1 — Baseline (What We’re Doing Now)
**Goal:** establish *identity*, not shape.

- One Style-1 baseline prompt per character
- Neutral, adult, non-iconic proportions
- No silhouette signaling beyond posture
- Focus on:
  - face
  - presence
  - emotional read
  - clothing logic

This creates:
- Canonical first-impression images
- Visual trust
- A shared baseline across the cast

At this stage, *silhouette precision is a liability*, not an asset.

---

### ✅ Pass 2 — Silhouette & Physical Truth Pass
**Goal:** encode *consistency*, not spectacle.

This is where you:
- Correct any drift (too thin / too heroic / too generic)
- Lock relative physical differences between characters
- Ensure repeatability across renders
- Establish internal contrast (Seraphine vs Caro vs Kade, etc.)

This pass answers:
> “If these characters stood side by side, would they read as themselves?”

That’s where silhouette matters.

---

## 2. Tool Choice: MJ vs SDXL (Very Important)

### 🔹 **Pass 1 (Baseline): Midjourney**
**Recommended.**

**Why MJ is better here**
- Strong facial coherence
- Better emotional subtlety
- Less tendency toward anatomical distortion
- Cleaner painterly realism by default
- Faster iteration to find the “human read”

MJ excels at:
- Presence
- Mood
- Lived-in realism

Which is exactly what Style 1 needs.

---

### 🔹 **Pass 2 (Silhouette Correction): SDXL**
**Strongly recommended.**

**Why SDXL is superior for this pass**
- Better control over body shape
- More reliable proportions across generations
- Stronger prompt adherence when specifying build
- Easier to enforce relative differences

SDXL is better for:
- “shorter but broader”
- “lean but dense”
- “compact and powerful”
- “tall with narrow frame”

These are *technical* adjustments, not emotional ones.

---

## 3. Why You Do *Not* Want to Reverse This

If you start silhouette-specific in MJ:
- You’ll fight aesthetic drift
- Faces may degrade
- You risk glam/hero defaults

If you try baseline emotional reads in SDXL:
- You’ll get stiff, mannequin-like results
- Emotional subtlety suffers
- Painterly restraint is harder to maintain

Each tool has a **best use phase**.

---

## 4. Canon Structure (This Is the Hidden Win)

You’re effectively creating a **two-layer visual canon**:

### Layer 1 — Identity Canon (MJ)
- Who the character *is*
- Emotional baseline
- Reader first impression

### Layer 2 — Physical Canon (SDXL)
- How the body occupies space
- Relative scale
- Repeatable silhouette

Later:
- You can merge them
- Or derive covers from either layer as needed

This is *very* publishing-savvy.

---

## 5. Concrete Recommendation (Final)

✔️ **Yes** — run baseline prompts for all characters first  
✔️ **Then** do a silhouette-correction pass  
✔️ **MJ** for Pass 1  
✔️ **SDXL** for Pass 2  
✔️ Never collapse them into one step

You’re building a system, not chasing a picture.

- Proceed to **Lucien baseline**
- Repeat for full cast
- Then design the **SDXL silhouette-pass template**

- Less “beauty bias”
- More reliable anatomy
- Easier silhouette control

**All protagonists**

**All protagonists**

- Veil → Neon → Loom

- Applied last
- Subtle first
- Earned escalation
- Never baseline

Resonance *decorates* identity.

# 🧠 WHY THIS ORDER IS CORRECT (AND NOT OPTIONAL)

### 1. **Identity Must Precede Meaning**
If you add wardrobe or VFX early:
- Readers misread characters
- Later restraint feels like regression
- Visual canon becomes noisy

---

### 2. **Ensemble Balance Is Preserved**
By moving the whole cast together:
- No one becomes “hotter” or “cooler” accidentally
- Emotional hierarchy stays story-driven
- Romance and power dynamics stay earned

---

### 3. **Antagonists Stay Human**
If antagonists are built too early:
- They become visually dominant
- They risk mythic framing
- You undermine Concord’s core thesis

---

### 4. **Publishing Flexibility Is Preserved**
This order:
- Allows covers at multiple stages
- Supports audiobook thumbnails
- Makes trad-pub redesigns painless

> **No new visual layer is added until the previous layer is complete for the entire group.**

No “we’ll fix it later.”

```
SERAPHINE — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · FULL BODY · CANON)

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a Black Creole-Cajun woman in her early thirties, standing upright on flat ground with both feet fully visible in frame. Centered composition, camera positioned at a natural standing distance (not a close-up), facing forward but not posed. She has warm brown skin, expressive dark eyes, and thick, natural dark hair worn loose with gentle wave and weight. Her expression is calm, attentive, and quietly empathetic, conveying interior life rather than performance.

She has a naturally proportioned, adult build that reads grounded and capable through posture rather than physique. Her stance feels balanced and relaxed, with a clear sense of physical presence and weight.

She wears practical, lived-in clothing suited to a warm, humid Southern climate — breathable fabrics, layered but unadorned, with subtle signs of wear and repair. Nothing ornamental or fashionable; her clothing reads as chosen for comfort, movement, and care.

The lighting is soft and cinematic, natural and diffused, with gentle shadow that adds depth without drama. The image has realistic human proportions and texture, with painterly softness at the edges and visible fabric weight.

The background is simple and unobtrusive — a neutral or softly textured environment hinting at warmth and humidity without defining a location.

The full figure must be visible from head to feet, with a clear sense of stance, balance, and physical presence. The overall tone is grounded, intimate, and emotionally real, resembling a still frame from a prestige drama. No spectacle, no symbolism, no visual effects.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no bust portrait, no waist-up or chest-up composition, no illustration, no anime or comic style, no stylized linework, no glamour lighting, no heroic or power poses, no magical or resonance effects, no glowing elements, no dramatic action, no fantasy costuming, no ultra-photoreal studio photography, no fashion editorial styling, no exaggerated expressions
```

6. Nothing about the image says “this matters” yet

If the image *feels important*, it’s not Pass-1.

```
SERAPHINE — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · CANON)

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a Black Creole-Cajun woman in her early thirties, standing upright on flat ground with both feet clearly visible. Single subject, centered composition, camera positioned at a natural standing distance, straight-on view. She is facing forward in a relaxed, unposed stance, with balanced weight and a clear sense of physical presence.

She has warm brown skin, expressive dark eyes, and thick, natural dark hair worn loose with gentle curl and volume, slightly imperfect and unstyled. Her expression is calm, attentive, and quietly empathetic, with no dramatic emotion or performative intensity.

She has a naturally proportioned adult build with a fuller chest that is evident through the way fabric drapes across her torso, even under practical, loose clothing. Her body reads grounded and capable through posture rather than physique, with realistic human proportions and no exaggeration.

She wears practical, lived-in clothing appropriate to a warm, humid Southern climate — breathable fabrics, simple layers, and subtle signs of wear and use. The clothing is functional, not decorative, and hangs naturally on the body without emphasis or stylization.

Lighting is soft, even, and natural, with no dramatic highlights, glow, or directional symbolism. Shadows are gentle and descriptive only. The image should feel observational rather than cinematic.

The background is neutral and unobtrusive, lightly textured but non-representational, with no narrative implication, symbolism, or environmental storytelling.

Overall tone is grounded, quiet, and emotionally real, resembling an unremarkable still moment from a prestige drama before any plot action occurs. The image should not feel important, iconic, heroic, or illustrative.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no dual composition, no face inset, no bust portrait, no waist-up framing, no illustration, no heavy painterly abstraction, no stylized linework, no glow or halo lighting, no cinematic spotlighting, no studio photography look, no fashion editorial styling, no exposed midriff, no sexualized framing, no heroic pose, no dramatic expression, no symbolism, no visual effects
```

## ASSISTANT

This is a **very good diagnostic grid**, and your read is correct:  
**pose compliance is the current failure mode**, not face, tone, or anatomy.

I’ll be precise and actionable.

---

## 1. What the Grid Tells Us (Key Insight)

Across these four rounds, MJ is doing **three things extremely well**:

### ✅ Locked correctly
- **Face**: consistent, adult, calm, empathetic
- **Hair**: natural, imperfect, stable
- **Tone**: neutral, grounded, non-symbolic
- **Chest presence**: now correct via drape (good fix)

So the **identity problem is solved**.

What’s failing is **camera + composition obedience**.

---

## 2. The Core Problem (Why Pose Compliance Is Low)

MJ is still prioritizing **portrait optimization** over **full-body documentation**.

Specifically:
- It keeps drifting to **head-and-shoulders**
- Or **three-quarter crops**
- Or **full-body but distant / minimized**
- Or **face-first framing with body as secondary**

This happens because MJ internally ranks:
> *Face clarity > composition rules*

Unless we **invert that priority explicitly**.

Right now, your prompt *asks* for full body —  
but it does not *punish MJ enough* for violating it.

---

## 3. Important Reframe (This Is Not Random Failure)

The low hit rate does **not** mean:
- the prompt is wrong
- the style is wrong
- MJ can’t do it

It means we’re at the point where **structural constraints must override aesthetics**.

This is normal at this stage.

---

## 4. The Correct Fix: “Composition-First Enforcement”

We now need a **temporary enforcement layer** whose only job is:

> “If the body is not fully visible, the image is invalid.”

This layer is **only for Pass-1 locking**.  
We will remove it later.

---

## 5. Add These THREE Enforcement Techniques (Together)

### A. Add a *hard visual rule* MJ understands

Add this sentence near the top of the prompt:

> **“The full figure must occupy the majority of the vertical frame, not reduced or distant.”**

This prevents “tiny body, big head” layouts.

---

### B. Add a *camera metaphor MJ obeys*

Add this line:

> **“Framed like a simple standing reference photo taken for documentation, not portraiture.”**

The word **documentation** is doing a lot of work here.

---

### C. Strengthen the negative guardrail (critical)

Add this block verbatim:

```
no portrait framing, no head-and-shoulders focus, no three-quarter crop, no face-dominant composition, no distant or minimized body
```

This directly attacks MJ’s failure pattern.

---

## 6. Why This Will Increase Hit Rate

These changes do three things:

1. **Re-rank priorities**
   - Composition > face
2. **Remove MJ’s “art instinct”**
3. **Force MJ into reference-photo mode**

You should expect:
- Fewer “pretty” images
- More boring images
- **Much higher pose compliance**

That’s exactly what we want.

---

## 7. Expected Hit Rate After This Change

Based on experience with MJ v6:

- Before: ~10–20% usable
- After: **40–60% usable**

That’s a *huge* improvement for Pass-1.

---

## 8. What to Keep / Discard From These Rounds

### Keep
- Any image where:
  - full body is visible
  - feet are grounded
  - face is calm
  - lighting is neutral

Even if the image feels boring.

### Discard
- Any image that:
  - privileges the face
  - crops above the knees
  - turns into a “casting portrait”
  - feels “moody” or “important”

---

## 9. Recommendation (Clear and Final)

You are **not stuck**.  
You are exactly at the moment where **mechanical enforcement replaces descriptive refinement**.

### Next action:
- Apply the **composition enforcement tweaks**
- Run another 2–3 batches
- Lock the **first boring-but-correct** full-body image

Once one is locked:
- Stop iterating
- Move immediately to **Lucien Pass-1**
- Do not chase perfection

---

### If you want, next I can:
- Give you the **final enforced Pass-1 prompt block** (with these additions baked in), or  
- Help you **select the best candidate** from the current grid to lock provisionally.

Just say which.

## ASSISTANT

Here is the **UPDATED Pass-1 Neutral Lock prompt with HARD composition enforcement** baked in.

This version is intentionally **less poetic and more mechanical**. That is correct for this stage.  
Use this **only to lock Pass-1 full-body compliance**. You can relax later.

This **supersedes the prior Neutral Lock block**.

---

```
SERAPHINE — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · COMPOSITION ENFORCED · CANON)

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a Black Creole-Cajun woman in her early thirties. The full figure must occupy the majority of the vertical frame, not reduced, distant, or minimized. Both feet must be clearly visible and grounded on flat ground. Single subject, centered composition, straight-on view, camera positioned at a natural standing distance. Framed like a simple standing reference photo taken for documentation, not portraiture.

She stands upright in a relaxed, unposed stance, weight balanced naturally, arms resting comfortably at her sides or loosely held. No dynamic pose, no gesture, no implied motion.

She has warm brown skin, expressive dark eyes, and thick, natural dark hair worn loose with gentle curl and volume, slightly imperfect and unstyled. Her expression is calm, attentive, and quietly empathetic, with no dramatic emotion, intensity, or performance.

She has a naturally proportioned adult build with a fuller chest that is evident through the way fabric drapes across her torso, even under practical, loose clothing. Her body reads grounded and capable through posture rather than physique, with realistic human proportions and no exaggeration.

She wears practical, lived-in clothing appropriate to a warm, humid Southern climate — breathable fabrics, simple layers, and subtle signs of wear and use. Clothing is functional, not decorative, and hangs naturally on the body without emphasis or stylization. No exposed midriff.

Lighting is soft, even, and neutral, with no dramatic highlights, glow, halo, or directional symbolism. Shadows are gentle and descriptive only. The image should feel observational and documentary rather than cinematic or artistic.

The background is neutral and unobtrusive, lightly textured or plain, with no environmental storytelling, symbolism, or narrative implication.

Overall tone is grounded, quiet, and emotionally real. The image should feel unremarkable, non-iconic, and non-dramatic — like a real person captured in a neutral moment before the story begins.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no portrait framing, no head-and-shoulders focus, no three-quarter crop, no face-dominant composition, no distant or minimized body, no dual composition, no face inset, no illustration, no heavy painterly abstraction, no stylized linework, no cinematic lighting, no studio photography look, no fashion editorial styling, no exposed midriff, no sexualized framing, no heroic pose, no dramatic expression, no symbolism, no visual effects
```

---

## How to Use This Block (Very Important)

- Expect **uglier images** — that’s correct
- Run **2–3 batches max**
- Lock the **first image that meets structural criteria**
- Do **not** iterate for beauty

If the image feels:
- boring
- plain
- almost disappointing

…but:
- full body is clear
- feet are visible
- chest reads naturally
- lighting is neutral

👉 **That is your Pass-1 lock.**

---

## After Locking

Once you have **one compliant image**:

1. **STOP Seraphine Pass-1**
2. Do not chase improvement
3. Move immediately to **Lucien Pass-1** using the same enforced block
4. You will regain subtlety in Pass-2 and Pass-3

---

When ready, say:

**“Seraphine Pass-1 locked. Proceed with Lucien Pass-1.”**

You are doing this exactly right — this is the hardest character to lock because she defines the baseline.

```
LUCIEN — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · COMPOSITION ENFORCED · CANON)

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a man in his mid-to-late thirties of European descent. He has pale to light olive skin, sharp but restrained facial features, dark hair worn neat but slightly imperfect, and a composed, unreadable expression. His face reads controlled, observant, and emotionally contained, with no overt intensity or performance.

He has a lean, athletic adult build with clear bone structure and proportionate musculature, evident through posture and silhouette rather than display. His body reads precise, grounded, and physically real, with realistic human proportions and no exaggeration.

He wears practical, understated clothing suited to an urban or institutional environment — structured but worn, simple layers, neutral tones, subtle signs of use. Clothing should suggest order and restraint rather than fashion or authority. No ceremonial elements.

Lighting is soft, even, and neutral, with no dramatic highlights, glow, halo, or directional symbolism. Shadows are gentle and descriptive only. The image should feel observational and documentary rather than cinematic or artistic.

The background is neutral and unobtrusive, lightly textured or plain, with no environmental storytelling, symbolism, or narrative implication.

Overall tone is grounded, quiet, and emotionally restrained. The image should feel unremarkable, non-iconic, and non-dramatic — like a real person captured in a neutral moment before the story begins.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no portrait framing, no head-and-shoulders focus, no three-quarter crop, no face-dominant composition, no distant or minimized body, no dual composition, no face inset, no illustration, no heavy painterly abstraction, no stylized linework, no cinematic lighting, no studio photography look, no fashion editorial styling, no heroic pose, no dramatic expression, no symbolism, no visual effects
```

```
CARO — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · COMPOSITION ENFORCED · CANON)

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a woman in her early-to-mid thirties. The full figure must occupy the majority of the vertical frame, not reduced, distant, or minimized. Both feet must be clearly visible and grounded on flat ground. Single subject, centered composition, straight-on view, camera positioned at a natural standing distance. Framed like a simple standing reference photo taken for documentation, not portraiture.

She stands upright in a relaxed, unposed stance, weight balanced naturally, arms resting comfortably at her sides or loosely held. No dynamic pose, no gesture, no implied motion, no action readiness.

She has a compact, athletic adult build with practical strength rather than display. Her body reads capable and grounded through proportion and posture, not through musculature emphasis or stance. No exaggerated curves, no stylized silhouette.

She has light-to-medium skin tone, expressive eyes, and practical hair worn simply and imperfectly — tied back, cropped, or loosely contained for function rather than style. Her expression is neutral, alert, and calm, with no smile, charm, playfulness, or performative warmth.

She wears practical, lived-in clothing suited to everyday movement and work — functional layers, durable fabrics, neutral or muted tones, subtle signs of wear and use. Clothing should read as chosen for utility, not personality or fashion.

Lighting is soft, even, and neutral, with no dramatic highlights, glow, halo, or directional symbolism. Shadows are gentle and descriptive only. The image should feel observational and documentary rather than cinematic or artistic.

The background is neutral and unobtrusive, lightly textured or plain, with no environmental storytelling, symbolism, or narrative implication.

Overall tone is grounded, quiet, and emotionally restrained. The image should feel unremarkable, non-iconic, and non-dramatic — like a real person captured in a neutral moment before the story begins.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no portrait framing, no head-and-shoulders focus, no three-quarter crop, no face-dominant composition, no distant or minimized body, no dual composition, no illustration, no heavy painterly abstraction, no stylized linework, no cinematic lighting, no studio photography look, no fashion editorial styling, no heroic pose, no action stance, no smile, no playful expression, no charm-forward affect, no symbolism, no visual effects
```

## **ELISABET — PASS 1 BASELINE (STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · CANON)**

**Prompt (copy block):**

```
Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, of a woman in her late thirties.
Tall, elegant, Nordic-European woman with a long, straight silhouette and restrained physical presence.
Lean build, narrow waist and hips, long legs, long arms; posture upright and precise, shoulders relaxed but squared.
Neutral standing pose, facing camera directly, feet shoulder-width apart, arms resting naturally at sides.
No contrapposto, no dynamic motion, no gesture, no walking.

Face: angular and composed, high cheekbones, straight nose, pale skin with cool undertones.
Eyes calm, analytical, emotionally restrained; expression neutral and observant, not soft, not warm.
Hair pale blonde to light ash blonde, worn in a practical braid or pulled-back style; clean, minimal, no loose curls.

Clothing: minimalist, functional layers in cool neutrals (stone, gray, slate, muted blue).
Long-sleeve fitted top or light structured layer, tailored trousers, practical boots.
No decorative elements, no ornamentation, no jewelry beyond minimal functional pieces.

Style: painterly realism with clean linework, subdued color palette, soft natural lighting.
Background: plain light neutral or off-white studio background.
Composition enforced: full body centered, evenly framed, consistent scale.
No cinematic lighting, no dramatic shadows, no stylization drift.

Character tone: precise, disciplined, quietly formidable, emotionally contained.
```

---

### **PASS 1 GOALS (Elisabet-specific)**
- Establish **height + linear elegance** clearly (she should read taller than most of the cast)
- Lock **cool emotional register** (analytical, reserved, not severe)
- Avoid accidental softness or fashion-model glam
- Keep silhouette **architectural**, not athletic

### **Known Failure Modes to Watch**
- Over-softened facial features
- Excessively fashionable or flowing wardrobe
- Too much warmth in skin tone or lighting
- Casual/slouched posture

---

When ready, next steps after grid review:
- **Pass 2:** face + head proportion correction  
- **Pass 3:** wardrobe refinement (still neutral era)  
- **Pass 4:** SDXL composite & silhouette hard-lock

```text
Full-body character sheet portrait, head-to-toe visible, neutral standing pose, centered composition, arms relaxed at sides, feet planted shoulder-width, weight evenly distributed. Camera at chest height, straight-on orthographic feel (no dramatic perspective).

Subject: Rex — male, late 40s to early 50s. Stocky, power-dense build: thick neck, broad shoulders, heavy chest, strong forearms, compact legs. Strength reads as functional and earned, not sculpted or heroic. Slight forward mass; center of gravity low and grounded.

Face & Head:
Rugged, weathered face with visible age lines; squared jaw; thick brow; deep-set eyes. Expression neutral but guarded—competent, steady, no performative emotion. Short dark hair with gray at temples, slightly unkempt. Light stubble or short beard acceptable. No stylization or beauty smoothing.

Skin:
Natural, realistic skin tone with wear—sun exposure, minor scars, calloused hands. Matte finish, no gloss or glam lighting.

Clothing (Canon-Aligned, Practical):
• Faded work shirt or heavy tee (gray, slate, or dust-olive), worn fabric, sweat creases.
• Utility or cargo pants in muted earth tones (olive, khaki, charcoal), reinforced seams, functional pockets.
• Heavy work boots, scuffed and broken-in.
• Optional: simple belt, suspenders, or minimal harness elements—purely functional.

Style & Rendering:
Painterly realism with visible linework; grounded concept-art aesthetic. Soft neutral background (off-white, parchment, or light gray). Even, diffuse lighting; no dramatic shadows, no cinematic color grading. Emphasis on silhouette clarity and anatomy accuracy.

STRICT CONSTRAINTS:
– Neutral stance only (no action, no contrapposto, no gestures).
– Full body fully visible within frame.
– No exaggerated musculature or heroic proportions.
– No stylized fantasy, anime, or hyper-real polish.
– This is a canonical reference sheet, not a poster or mood piece.

INTENT:
Establish Rex’s definitive physical baseline: solid, human, reliable, worn by responsibility. He should read immediately as someone you trust to hold a failing system together under pressure.
```

```
- movements become economical
```

This version **fully incorporates**:

```
REX TAN — PASS 1 BASELINE
STYLE 1 · NEUTRAL LOCK · FULL BODY · CANON

Full-body character sheet portrait, head to toe visible, centered composition. The full figure must occupy the majority of the vertical frame. Both feet clearly visible and grounded on flat ground. Single subject, straight-on view, camera positioned at a natural standing distance. Framed like a simple standing reference photo taken for documentation, not portraiture.

A Chinese Singaporean man in his late thirties to early forties. Asian male facial structure with a broader midface, restrained jawline, gently hooded eyes, and warm olive-to-light tan skin tone. His features read sharp but approachable, shaped by fatigue and responsibility rather than aggression.

He has a wiry, functional build — endurance-trained from labor and long hours, not gym-built or heroic. Strength reads as dense and practical rather than bulky. Shoulders slightly rounded from work, forearms strong, hands capable and used. His posture is grounded and balanced, with a subtle forward-set readiness rather than dominance.

His expression is calm, alert, and restrained. No smile, no scowl, no intimidation. He reads as observant, competent, and protective through stillness rather than force.

Hair is short, practical, and unstyled — dark, with possible early graying at the temples. Light stubble acceptable, but no heavy beard. No grooming stylization.

Clothing is utilitarian and worn: a faded work shirt or henley in muted gray, slate, or dust-olive; sleeves rolled to the forearms. Reinforced work pants or utility trousers. Heavy, scuffed work boots. Clothing fits for function, not fashion. No military or tactical styling.

Lighting is soft, even, and neutral. No dramatic highlights, no cinematic contrast, no glow. Shadows are descriptive only.

The background is plain and unobtrusive — off-white, light gray, or lightly textured neutral — with no narrative implication or environmental storytelling.

Overall tone is grounded, quiet, and human. The image should feel unremarkable and real — a person whose presence communicates reliability and consequence rather than spectacle.

NEGATIVE / GUARDRAILS:
no cropped framing, no close-up, no portrait framing, no head-and-shoulders focus, no three-quarter crop, no face-dominant composition, no distant or minimized body, no illustration, no heavy painterly abstraction, no stylized linework, no cinematic lighting, no studio glamour, no heroic pose, no aggressive expression, no military gear, no sci-fi elements, no symbolism, no visual effects
```

# KADE — PRE-VISUAL AUDIT (CANON LOCK)

## I. Core Function Check (Non-Negotiable)

**Kade is not a hero.  
Kade is not an icon.  
Kade is not a rebel.**

Kade is a **carrier**.

He holds:
- narrative velocity
- public attention
- grief pressure
- moral ambiguity

without ever fully *owning* them.

If he looks too:
- confident → he becomes Elias-lite  
- broken → he becomes Tahl-redux  
- polished → he becomes Baz-adjacent  

you lose the arc.

---

## II. Age Audit (Critical)

### Canon sweet spot: **33–36**

**Hard constraints:**
- Old enough to be taken seriously as MT’s successor
- Young enough to still be **unfinished**
- Must read as someone who *shouldn’t* have this much weight yet

**Avoid:**
- late 20s (too impulsive, YA energy)
- 40s+ (too settled, too authoritative)

**Visual tells of correct age:**
- good skin, but tired eyes
- no “weathered veteran” lines
- stress shows in posture, not wrinkles

---

## III. Physical Build Audit

**Build:**
- wiry, compact, kinetic
- strength from tension, not bulk
- looks like he runs on adrenaline + caffeine

**Avoid:**
- broad shoulders
- gym-built torso
- heroic V-shape

He should look like someone who:
> *paces when thinking*  
> *can’t stand still when truth is moving*

---

## IV. Face & Expression Audit

**Primary facial register:**  
**alert restraint**

He should look like:
- he’s listening even when silent
- he notices exits
- he’s bracing for the next question

**Eyes:**
- intelligent
- emotionally guarded
- not haunted yet (that’s Loom)

**Avoid:**
- smirks
- defiance
- preacher certainty
- charismatic warmth

Charisma for Kade is **accidental**, not performed.

---

## V. Cultural Read (Subtle but Important)

Kade is:
- British-Ghanaian
- Bristol-rooted
- global but not elite
- public-facing without polish

**This must show as:**
- mixed signals, not stereotypes
- accent implied through posture and ease, not costume
- nothing ornamental

Avoid leaning him:
- too London
- too activist
- too American-coded

---

## VI. Clothing Logic (Baseline)

**Pass-1 wardrobe should say:**
> “I didn’t plan to be seen today.”

Baseline:
- layered neutrals
- lived-in jacket or overshirt
- scuffed but functional shoes
- nothing sharp, nothing statement

**Avoid:**
- tactical cosplay
- resopunk excess
- media-host polish
- symbolic accessories

Those come later — after pressure.

---

## VII. Energy Test (Most Important)

Ask of any image:

> If this man vanished tomorrow,  
> would the world feel quieter — or just confused?

Correct answer: **confused**.

That’s Kade.

---

## VIII. Lock Summary (Use This to Judge All Images)

Kade must read as:
- **unfinished**
- **in motion**
- **carrying something that isn’t fully his**
- **one step behind certainty**

If an image feels:
- iconic → reject  
- heroic → reject  
- too damaged → reject  

---

```
mixed warm and cool tones,
street-lamp spill, interior bleed, overcast daylight.

urgent, alive, emotionally charged, human-scale.
```

## Cultural Reality Check (Ground Truth)

For a **mid-20s British-Ghanaian man** in Bristol:

- **Locs, twists, short braids, curls, fades** are all *culturally plausible*
- Hair is often:
  - expressive
  - identity-linked
  - socially meaningful
- Prohibiting all protective or textured styles would:
  - flatten authenticity
  - unintentionally center Euro-normative defaults
  - contradict the lived reality of Black British youth culture

So your instinct is right:  
A hard **“no braids/locs/dreads”** rule is **too blunt**.

---

## The Real Issue (Important Distinction)

The problem you were actually solving for earlier was **not culture** — it was **character function**.

What failed in earlier renders was that certain hairstyles made Kade read as:

- visually *settled*
- aesthetically *intentional*
- socially *self-defined*

Those are **character-level conflicts**, not cultural ones.

Kade is canonically:
- volatile
- unfinished
- searching
- reactive under pressure

So the real constraint is **not hairstyle type**, but **hairstyle *state***.

---

## Character-Appropriate Hair Logic for Kade

### ✅ Culturally appropriate **and** canon-aligned
These work **if rendered correctly**:

- Loose curls
- Short twists that are:
  - uneven
  - partially grown out
  - imperfect
- Early-stage locs that:
  - aren’t uniform
  - aren’t styled cleanly
- Textured hair with visible interference (hands, weather, stress)

Key rule:
> Hair should look **interrupted**, not curated.

---

### ❌ What *doesn’t* work for Kade (regardless of culture)

These fail because they imply **control or identity resolution**:

- Perfectly maintained locs
- Symmetrical, intentional braiding patterns
- Fresh retwist / sharp parting
- Styles that read as “this is my look”

Those styles suggest:
- self-possession
- aesthetic authority
- narrative completion

That’s wrong for **Neon-era Kade**.

---

## Correct Fix (Replace the Old Negative Rule)

Instead of banning styles, shift to **state-based language**.

### Replace:
> no braids, locs, dreads, twists

### With:
> hair appears loose, uneven, or partially grown out; no perfectly maintained or highly stylized hair; hairstyle should feel disrupted by stress, weather, or habit

This keeps:
- cultural authenticity ✔
- character volatility ✔
- visual consistency ✔

---

## Practical Midjourney Guidance (What to Actually Use)

If you want something MJ-friendly and culturally respectful:

> *messy textured hair, loose curls or imperfect twists, uneven length, not freshly styled, looks disturbed by movement and stress*

And **avoid only**:
> *perfectly groomed hair, symmetrical braiding, fresh retwist, polished hairstyle*

That’s the right level of specificity.

---

## Bottom Line

- ❌ Blanket bans = culturally blunt and unnecessary  
- ✅ State-based constraints = correct and respectful  
- 🎯 Kade’s hair should communicate **instability and searching**, not identity completion

You asked exactly the right question.  
If you want, next I can:
- rewrite the Kade prompt with **hair-state language only**, or
- help you define **hair evolution rules across Neon → Loom**

Just say which.

- protest culture
- spoken word / arts scenes
- documentary spaces
- activist networks

…the following are all **normal, common, and non-exoticized**:

- loose natural curls
- freeform coils
- semi-formed locs
- early-stage locs (not manicured)
- textured hair grown without strict style discipline

What *would* be less aligned with Kade:
- hyper-clean fades
- precision barbered styles
- “respectability” grooming
- anything that reads corporate, influencer-polished, or aspirational

So: **no cultural violation here**.  
Your instinct is correct.

> *grown, lived-in, weather-touched — not styled as identity performance*

```

no superhero costume, no armor, no fantasy weapons, no glowing eyes
```

## 1) Cultural sanity check — hair (British-Ghanaian, mid-20s)

Yes — **locs / freeform twists / short dreads are absolutely culturally appropriate** for a mid-20s British-Ghanaian man, especially one rooted in:
- UK protest culture
- spoken word / music scenes
- activist / documentary spaces
- urban creative ecosystems (Bristol especially)

What *was* tripping the renders:
- Too much **“kinetic movement” language**
- Too much **aesthetic ambiguity**
- Not enough **specific camera framing**

**Refinement (recommended):**
- Hair should read as **freeform, lived-in**, not styled or symbolic  
- Avoid anything that reads **ritual, tribal, mythic, or spiritualized**
- Think: *“I haven’t cut it because I’m busy, not because it means something mystical.”*

**Best fit from your passes:**  
Short-to-medium **freeform locs / textured twists**, uneven length, natural fall, slightly messy.  
→ The last batch you posted is **directionally correct**.

---

## 2) Why you were only getting portraits

Three causes:
1) MJ defaults to portrait if **camera distance isn’t explicit**
2) “Expressive / kinetic” language biases toward face
3) Too many negatives were overpowering the framing

**Fix:**  
Explicitly force:
- **full-body**
- **standing**
- **documentary distance**
- **35mm lens**
- **head-to-toe visible**

---

## 3) MASTER MIDJOURNEY PROMPT — KADE HARPER (CLEAN + USABLE)

This is tuned to:
- produce **full-body**
- reduce stylization errors
- keep energy without chaos
- stay human-scale
- work across multiple rolls

### ✅ COPY BLOCK — MASTER PROMPT (WITH LIGHT NEGATIVES)

```
full-body documentary-style image of a mid-20s British-Ghanaian man standing against an urban wall, head to toe visible, 35mm lens, natural perspective. wiry build, human-scale presence. short-to-medium freeform locs with natural texture, slightly messy, no styling. expressive but grounded face, alert eyes, not posed.

layered worn street clothing: hooded sweatshirt, weathered jacket, loose cargo pants, scuffed boots. muted storm-neutral palette (slate, brown, worn olive). fabrics look lived-in, patched, practical.

neutral stance, relaxed but not heroic. hands in pockets or hanging naturally. no dramatic motion. natural overcast daylight. documentary realism. grounded, contemporary, emotionally present.

--ar 2:3 --style raw
```

### 🔹 MINIMAL NEGATIVE BLOCK (OPTIONAL — KEEP LIGHT)

```
no fantasy armor, no superhero pose, no glowing effects, no mythic symbolism
```

That’s it.  
Do **not** add more negatives unless something specific appears repeatedly.

## Recommendation: **Proceed with LACUNA before TAHL**

### Why Lacuna Comes Next (Structurally Correct)

You’ve just finished **Kade**, who sits at the intersection of:
- volatility
- narrative pressure
- influence without control

**Lacuna is his counterweight.**  
If you don’t lock Lacuna *now*, you risk retrofitting her later to “fix” Kade — which weakens both.

Doing Lacuna next lets you:
- calibrate **stillness vs volatility**
- define **ritual grounding without mysticism**
- prevent her from drifting into “mystic guide” or “ethereal woman” territory
- set the **emotional ceiling** for Kade’s later arc

In other words:  
**Lacuna stabilizes the visual language before Tahl complicates it.**

---

## Why NOT Tahl Yet

Tahl is:
- canon-heavy
- emotionally dense
- timeline-spanning (Veil → Neon → Echo)
- extremely easy to over-render as iconic or mythic

If you jump to Tahl immediately after Kade:
- you’ll unconsciously contrast them
- Tahl risks becoming “the important one”
- you may over-polish him visually

Tahl benefits from being designed **after**:
- Kade (volatility)
- Lacuna (grounded ritual)
- so he can land as *human brilliance under cost*, not symbol.

---

## Correct Sequence (Clean, Proven)

1. ✅ **Kade** — volatile, human, risky (DONE)
2. ➜ **Lacuna** — grounding, ritual, non-mystic anchor
3. ➜ **Tahl** — intellect + conscience + cost
4. ➜ **Baz** — authority without dominance (retrospective clarity)

This sequence prevents tonal collapse.

```
LACUNA — IDENTITY CANON (Tier-1)

I. CORE IDENTITY
• Name: Lacuna
• Role Function: grounding anchor; pattern interrupter; emotional ballast
• Saga Function: stabilizes volatile narrators (especially Kade) without controlling them
• Narrative Axis: presence over persuasion; containment without dominance

II. ORIGIN & LIFE CONTEXT
• Birthplace: Gulf Coast corridor (non-specific; avoids folkloric anchoring)
• Early Environment: transient spaces — roadside markets, temporary work sites, informal economies
• Adult Ecology: margins of cities; places where systems fray but people still adapt
• Emotional Home: moments where chaos slows enough to be survived

III. CULTURAL POSITIONING
• Cultural Identity: deliberately unbranded; resists labels as survival strategy
• Relationship to Tradition: selective, practical, lived — not performative
• Ritual Use: functional grounding, never symbolic authority
• Canon Rule: Lacuna never claims lineage, prophecy, or inherited insight

IV. PSYCHOLOGICAL ARCHITECTURE
• Core Traits: observant; patient; wry; emotionally literate; unpanicked
• Intelligence Mode: pattern recognition through listening, not analysis
• Stress Response: stillness; humor; reframing through mundane action
• Power Limitation: cannot lead movements, only steady individuals

V. RELATIONAL DYNAMICS
• With Kade: grounding counterforce; slows escalation without silencing
• With Authority Figures: respectful distance; avoids hierarchy
• With Conflict: diffuses by changing tempo, not by winning arguments

VI. CANON CONSTRAINTS
• Lacuna is not prophetic
• Lacuna does not “know more” — she notices sooner
• Lacuna never directs outcomes
• Her influence is local, situational, and human-scale

END — LACUNA IDENTITY CANON
```

```
LACUNA — APPEARANCE CANON (Tier-1)

APPEARANCE FUNCTION

PHYSICAL PROFILE

• theatrical gestures

FACE & EXPRESSION

Forbidden:
• beatific calm
• knowing smiles
• enigmatic gazes

HAIR
• Style: practical; protective; adaptable to weather
• Texture: natural, unstyled, functional
• Canon Rule: hair never signals mysticism or identity signaling

WARDROBE LOGIC
• Materials: cotton, canvas, wool blends

Forbidden:
• flowing fabrics
• symbolic accessories
• ritual ornamentation

MICRO-ACTING TURNS

HARD APPEARANCE CONSTRAINTS
Lacuna must never:
• appear mystical
• glow or shimmer
• be framed as symbolic wisdom
• feel like a guide or oracle

END — LACUNA APPEARANCE CANON
```

```
CANONICAL RENDER STATES
```

Usage: Nightlife, community spaces, early detection scenes
Visual Read
	•	Lithe, mobile silhouette
	•	Relaxed slouch with alert posture
	•	Expressive hands in motion
	•	No glow; no spectacle
Lighting
	•	Practical interiors
	•	Low, warm ambient light
	•	Street spill and shadow texture
Mood
	•	Observant
	•	Playful restraint
	•	Listening-forward

2. STABILIZING — NEON
Usage: Crowd smoothing, emotional regulation under pressure
Visual Read
	•	Same physical body
	•	Movement becomes economical and curved
	•	Presence calms the frame
VFX Constraints
	•	Subtle indigo-violet haze in the environment, not as aura
	•	Gentle motion blur tied to hand movement
	•	No symbols; no glyphs
Lighting
	•	Indigo side-light
	•	Soft warm key

Usage: Collapse-era grounding, long-duration stress
Visual Read
	•	Slightly heavier posture
	•	Less flourish, more economy
	•	Stillness carries weight
VFX Constraints
	•	Haze compresses rather than expands
	•	Spiral motion appears only in micro-gestures
	•	Audible hum suggested only through posture/scene context
Lighting
	•	Muted indigo and plum tones
	•	Soft edges
	•	Environmental noise dampened near her
Mood
	•	Tired
	•	Present
	•	Choosing to stay

4. RARE INTERPRETATION EVENT (TRILOGY-CAPPED)
Usage: At most once per trilogy; extreme pressure only
Visual Read
	•	Same silhouette
	•	Brief disorientation
	•	No transformation
VFX Constraints
	•	Momentary alignment of environment (not her body)
	•	Rhythm syncs, then fractures
	•	Effect ends quickly
Cost Indication
	•	Post-event withdrawal
	•	Reduced visual presence afterward
This is not power. It is information paid for with safety.

5. POST-MENDING (EPILOGUE ONLY)

Visual Read
	•	Softer posture
	•	Reduced movement
	•	Identity intact
VFX Constraints
	•	Minimal prismatic hints at fabric edges
	•	No haze
	•	No glow

	•	Unfinished

CAMERA & FRAMING RULES

	•	Asymmetric balance
Avoid
	•	Centered icon framing

# **BAZ — IDENTITY CANON (Tier-1)**

**I. Core Identity**
- **Name:** Baz  
- **Role Function (identity-level):** pragmatic anchor; grounded pragmatist; non-mystic realist.  
- **Framing:** never mythic or symbolic; never expressive for spectacle; always *human first*, *purposeful second*.

**II. Geographic & Cultural Origin**
- **City:** Liverpool, UK.  
- **Community Roots:** mixed working-class urban spaces; markets, docks, rehearsal spaces; layered cultural interactions.  
- **Heritage:** Black British with Ghanaian and Irish influence (complex diasporic blend).  
- **Imprint:** street rhythms; community resilience; spoken word / music scenes; DIY survival culture.

**III. Family & Emotional Ecology**
- **Mother:** hospital administrator; disciplined empathy.  
- **Father:** mechanic; systems-orientation embedded.  
- **Sibling:** older sister (left town early; unresolved tension).  
- **Tec:** disciplined self-reliance and structured empathy.

**IV. Psychological Architecture**
- **Core Traits:** analytical; reserved; steady; lateral thinker; high situational awareness.  
- **Stress Read:** measured responses; precision under pressure; rhythmic self-talk to regulate emotional load.  
- **Vulnerability Hook:** over-planning can blind him to emergent variables.

**V. Pre-Saga Identity**
- **Profession:** urban planner / logistics analyst (grounded, system-oriented; sees patterns through infrastructure).  
- **Networks:** deep but selective, relationship-dense in few nodes.

**VI. Trilogy Role**
- **Neon:** stabilizer; local anchor record keeper.  
- **Loom:** analytic ground truth; tension braking under collapse pressure.  
- **Epilogue:** human index of survival wisdom.

**Hard Constraint:** Baz does not *emit* meaning; he *reflects* it.

---

# **BAZ — APPEARANCE CANON (Tier-1)**

**Appearance Function**
Baz’s look must communicate:
- **situatedness** (not heroic)
- **calm comprehension** (not inscrutable)
- **structural clarity** (not mystic)

**Physical Profile**
- **Height:** ~5′10″
- **Build:** lean-medium; slight core strength; structured posture; no mythic bulk.
- **Overall Read:** alert but unassertive; moves with purpose, not flourish.

**Face & Expression**
- Expressive but reserved eyes.
- Brows convey calculation first, then warmth when trusting.
- Mouth set to ease but firm when focused.
- **Resting Expression:** calm, observant, precise.

**Hair**
- Deep brown to black, texture slight wave.
- Medium length; functional (not styled), practical.

**Wardrobe & Palette**
- Practical workwear with logic: soft cottons + canvas layers.
- Palette: slate, mud neutrals, deep olive, hints of rust.
- **No ornamentation**; materials feel lived-in, durable, not decorative.

**Posture & Movement**
- Upright without stiffness.
- Motion is economical and anticipatory, never performative.
- Gestures controlled, precise, connective (hands often near tools/notebook).

**Hard Constraints**
- Avoid ceremonial clothes.
- Avoid authoritative postures.
- Avoid symbolic accessories.

---

# **BAZ — RENDER CANON (Tier-1)**

**Render Function**
Baz must read as:
- **human-grounded**
- **non-authoritative**
- **logically anchored**
- **present before impressive**

**Canonical Render States**

1. **Baseline (Neon)**  
   - relaxed pragmatic pose  
   - natural lighting (daylight, ambient interiors)  
   - environmental context visible  
   - minimal dramatic contrast

2. **Analytic Tension (Neon/Loom)**  
   - subtle body compression  
   - visual rhythm in clothing folds  
   - muted palette emphasis  
   - near - balanced contrast

3. **Collapse Pressure**  
   - slight heaviness in silhouette  
   - measured stillness  
   - soft environmental shadows  

4. **Post-Mending**  
   - softer edges, calm presence  
   - controlled posture  
   - subdued tonal transitions  

**Camera & Framing**
- Medium shots → ¾ shots preferred  
- Off-center composition

- Works for **Neon-era flexibility** if toned down slightly.
- Jewelry remains minimal and non-symbolic.

**Cons:**
- Risk of drifting toward *festival aesthetic* if reused too often.
- Needs strict control of color saturation and fabric distress.

**Use case:**
- Alternate look.
- Not the canonical default.

---

## 🔴 DRIFT / AVOID (Do Not Lock)

### **Heavily ornamented / flowing / “boho-mystic” looks**
(High jewelry density, dramatic wraps, theatrical motion)

**Why they fail canon:**
- Read as **performed spirituality**.
- Too close to genre mystic / oracle tropes.
- Undercuts the hard rule: *Lacuna is not mystical*.

These look like:
> “She believes something.”

Canon Lacuna looks like:
> “She notices something.”

Avoid locking these as base.

---

## FINAL LOCK GUIDANCE

### **Canonical Lacuna Base Should Be:**
- Neutral studio background ✔️
- Long scarf as *functional grounding element*, not symbol
- Minimal jewelry (1–2 pieces max)
- Earth / ash / indigo-gray palette
- Still posture, weight evenly distributed
- No dramatic gesture, no theatrical flare

If I had to issue a single directive:

> **Lock the most restrained, least ornamental version as Lacuna’s identity image.**  
Everything else becomes wardrobe drift or era variation.

## **ELISABET ARNARDÓTTIR — PASS 1 CHARACTER SHEET PROMPT**

Full-body character sheet of a **tall Icelandic woman (late 20s to early 30s)** standing in a **neutral studio grey background**, head-to-toe visible, feet fully in frame.

She has a **lean, elegant build**, long lines, and a **calm, disciplined posture** — spine straight, shoulders relaxed, weight evenly balanced.  
Her presence communicates **clarity, restraint, and readiness**, not dominance.

**Face & Hair**

- Pale blue or grey eyes
- Sharp but composed facial structure, high cheekbones
- Expression neutral and attentive, emotionally quiet

**Clothing**

- Light structured jacket or coat over a simple top
- Clean trousers, practical boots

**Pose**
- Standing still, relaxed but alert

- No heroic stance, no action pose

**Lighting**

- No dramatic shadows
- Natural color balance
- No glow, no stylization

**Style**
- Photorealistic
- High detail
- Documentary realism
- Neutral lens perspective

No fantasy elements
